}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$}}\kern 0.43057pt\psi

Algebraic entailment

Def.
0.3.3

\varphi\Vdash\psi

Semantic entailment

Def.
0.3.7

\varphi\Vdash_{\mathsf{LSpace}}\psi

Topological semantic entailment

Def.
0.3.19

\varphi\models\psi

First-order entailment

Sec.
0.3.4

Table 1:
Different notions of entailment.

0.3.1
Logic and Algebraic Semantics

Let
\mathbf{L}(\Prop)
denote the language generated by the grammar

\varphi::=p\mid\top\mid\bot\mid\varphi\wedge\varphi\mid\varphi\vee\varphi,

where
p
ranges over some arbitrary but fixed set
\Prop
of proposition
letters.
If no confusion arises we omit reference to
\Prop
and simply
write
\mathbf{L}
.
We define logics based on
\mathbf{L}

as a collection of
consequence pairs
,
similar to e.g.
[
15
]
.
A consequence pair is an expressions of the form
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi

where
\varphi
and
\psi
are formulae in
\mathbf{L}
,
and intuitively means: “If
\varphi
holds, then
so does
\psi
.”

0.3.1 Definition
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}
be the smallest set of consequence pairs
closed under the following axioms and rules:

\displaystyle p\trianglelefteqslant\top,

\displaystyle\qquad\bot\trianglelefteqslant p,

top
and
bottom

\displaystyle p\trianglelefteqslant p,

\displaystyle\qquad\frac{p\trianglelefteqslant q\quad q\trianglelefteqslant r}{p\trianglelefteqslant r},

reflexivity
and
transitivity

\displaystyle p\wedge q\trianglelefteqslant p,\qquad p\wedge q\trianglelefteqslant q,

\displaystyle\qquad\dfrac{r\trianglelefteqslant p\quad r\trianglelefteqslant q}{r\trianglelefteqslant p\wedge q},

conjunction rules

\displaystyle p\trianglelefteqslant p\vee q,\qquad q\trianglelefteqslant p\vee q,

\displaystyle\qquad\dfrac{p\trianglelefteqslant r\quad q\trianglelefteqslant r}{p\vee q\trianglelefteqslant r}

disjunction rules

If
\Gamma
is a set of consequence pairs then we let

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}(\Gamma)
denote the smallest set of consequence pairs
closed under uniform substitution, the axioms and rules mentioned above and those in
\Gamma
.
We write
\varphi\vdash_{\Gamma}\psi
if
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}(\Gamma)

and
\varphi\dashv\vdash_{\Gamma}\psi
if both
\varphi\vdash_{\Gamma}\psi

and
\psi\vdash_{\Gamma}\varphi
.
If
\Gamma
is the empty set then we simply write

\varphi\vdash\psi
and
\varphi\dashv\vdash\psi
.

The algebraic semantics of the logic
\mathbf{L}
are simply lattices.
We establish this formally.

0.3.2 Definition
.

Let
A
be a lattice with operations
\top_{A},\bot_{A},\wedge_{A},\vee_{A}
,
and induced order
\leq_{A}
.
A
lattice model
is a pair
{A}=(A,\sigma)

consisting of a lattice
A
and an assignment
\sigma:\Prop\to A

of the proposition letters.
The assignment
\sigma
uniquely extends to a map

\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\cdot\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{{A}}:\mathbf{L}\to A

by interpreting connectives with their lattice counterparts.

We say that a lattice
A

validates
a consequence pair
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi

if
\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\varphi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{{A}}\leq_{A}\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\psi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{{A}}
for
all lattice models
{A}
based on
A
, notation:
A\mathrel{\kern 0.43057pt\text{\hbox to7.06pt{\vbox to7.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.10971pt\lower-3.76735pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.92 L 0 4.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.55 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 -1.95 -1.67 -3.77 -0.08 -4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 1.95 -1.67 3.77 -0.08 4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$}}\kern 0.43057pt\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
.
If
\Gamma
is a set of consequence pairs then we write

\mathsf{Lat}(\Gamma)
for the full subcategory of
\mathsf{Lat}
whose objects
validate all consequence pairs in
\Gamma
.

0.3.3 Definition
.

Let
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi\}
be a set of consequence pairs.
Write
\varphi\mathrel{\kern 0.43057pt\text{\hbox to7.06pt{\vbox to7.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.10971pt\lower-3.76735pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.92 L 0 4.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.55 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 -1.95 -1.67 -3.77 -0.08 -4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 1.95 -1.67 3.77 -0.08 4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}${}_{\Gamma}$}}\kern 0.43057pt\psi
if

\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\varphi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{{A}}\leq_{A}\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\psi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{{A}}
for
every lattice model
{A}=(A,\sigma)
with
A\in\mathsf{Lat}(\Gamma)
.
We abbreviate
\varphi\mathrel{\kern 0.43057pt\text{\hbox to7.06pt{\vbox to7.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.10971pt\lower-3.76735pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.92 L 0 4.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.55 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 -1.95 -1.67 -3.77 -0.08 -4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 1.95 -1.67 3.77 -0.08 4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}${}_{\emptyset}$}}\kern 0.43057pt\psi

to
\varphi\mathrel{\kern 0.43057pt\text{\hbox to7.06pt{\vbox to7.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.10971pt\lower-3.76735pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.92 L 0 4.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.55 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 -1.95 -1.67 -3.77 -0.08 -4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 1.95 -1.67 3.77 -0.08 4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$}}\kern 0.43057pt\psi
.

Observe that
\dashv\vdash_{\Gamma}
is an equivalence relation on
\mathbf{L}
.
Write
L(\Gamma)
for the set of
\dashv\vdash_{\Gamma}
-equivalence classes
of
\mathbf{L}
, and denote by
[\varphi]
the equivalence class of
\varphi
in

L(\Gamma)
.
Then it follows from the rules in Definition
0.3.1
that

L(\Gamma)
carries a lattice structure, where

\top_{L}=[\top]
,
\bot_{L}=[\bot]
,

[\varphi]\wedge_{L}[\psi]=[\varphi\wedge\psi]
and

[\varphi]\wedge_{L}[\psi]=[\varphi\vee\psi]
.
Moreover,
L(\Gamma)
is in
\mathsf{Lat}(\Gamma)
, and
setting
\sigma_{L}:\Prop\to L(\Gamma):p\mapsto[p]
yields
lattice model
{L}_{\Gamma}=(L(\Gamma),\sigma_{L})
which acts as
the Lindenbaum-Tarski algebra. It follows from induction on the
structure of
\varphi
that
\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\varphi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{{L}_{\Gamma}}=[\varphi]

for all
\mathbf{L}
-formulae
\varphi
.

0.3.4 Lemma
.

We have
\varphi\mathrel{\kern 0.43057pt\text{\hbox to7.06pt{\vbox to7.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.10971pt\lower-3.76735pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.92 L 0 4.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.55 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 -1.95 -1.67 -3.77 -0.08 -4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 1.95 -1.67 3.77 -0.08 4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}${}_{\Gamma}$}}\kern 0.43057pt\psi
if and only if

\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\varphi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{{L}_{\Gamma}}\leq_{L}\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\psi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{{L}_{\Gamma}}
.

0.3.5 Proof
.

The “only if” holds by definition.
Conversely, if
A\in\mathsf{Lat}(\Gamma)
and
{A}=(A,\sigma_{A})

is a lattice model, then the assignment
[p]\mapsto\sigma_{A}(p)

extends to a lattice homomorphism
i:{L}_{\Gamma}\to{A}

such that
[\varphi]=\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\varphi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{{A}}
.
(This is well defined because
A
validates all consequence pairs in
\Gamma
.)
Then
\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\varphi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{{L}_{\Gamma}}\leq_{L}\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\psi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{{L}_{\Gamma}}

implies
[\varphi]\leq_{L}[\psi]
. Monotonicity of
i
yields

\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\varphi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{{A}}\leq_{L}\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\psi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{{A}}
,
hence
\varphi\mathrel{\kern 0.43057pt\text{\hbox to7.06pt{\vbox to7.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.10971pt\lower-3.76735pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.92 L 0 4.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.55 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 -1.95 -1.67 -3.77 -0.08 -4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 1.95 -1.67 3.77 -0.08 4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}${}_{\Gamma}$}}\kern 0.43057pt\psi
.

0.3.5 Theorem
.

We have
\varphi\vdash_{\Gamma}\psi
if and only if
\varphi\mathrel{\kern 0.43057pt\text{\hbox to7.06pt{\vbox to7.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.10971pt\lower-3.76735pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.92 L 0 4.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.55 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 -1.95 -1.67 -3.77 -0.08 -4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 1.95 -1.67 3.77 -0.08 4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}${}_{\Gamma}$}}\kern 0.43057pt\psi
.

0.3.6 Proof
.

By Lemma
0.3.4
it suffices to show that

\varphi\vdash_{\Gamma}\psi
if and only if

\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\varphi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{{L}_{\Gamma}}\leq_{L}\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\psi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{{L}_{\Gamma}}
.
It follows from the conjunction rules, reflexivity and transitivity that

\varphi\vdash_{\Gamma}\psi
if and only if

\varphi\wedge\psi\dashv\vdash_{\Gamma}\varphi
.
Therefore we have
\varphi\vdash_{\Gamma}\psi
if and only if

[\varphi\wedge\psi]=[\varphi]
in
{L}_{\Gamma}
,
and since
[\varphi\wedge\psi]=[\varphi]\wedge_{L}[\psi]
this holds
if and only if
[\varphi]\leq_{L}[\psi]
.
Recalling that
[\varphi]=\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\varphi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{{L}_{\Gamma}}
completes the proof.

0.3.2
Frame Semantics

The collection of filters of a semilattice forms a lattice.
Therefore we can use semilattices as frame semantics of weak positive logic,
with filters serving as denotations of formulae.
If moreover the semilattice is a lattice, then we can also use
principal filters as denotations of formulae.

0.3.7 Definition
.

A
lattice model
or
L-model
is a semilattice

(X,1,\curlywedge)
together with a valuation

V:\Prop\to\mathcal{F}(X,1,\curlywedge)
which assigns to each proposition
letter a filter of
(X,1,\curlywedge)
.
An L-model
(X,1,\curlywedge,V)
is called
principal
if

(X,1,\curlywedge)
has a bottom element
0
and binary joins denoted by

\curlyvee
(so it forms a lattice)
and
V(p)
is a principal filter for all
p\in\Prop
.

The interpretation of an
\mathbf{L}
-formula
\varphi
at a state
x
in
a (principal) L-model
\mathfrak{M}=(X,1,\curlywedge,V)
is defined recursively via

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M},x\Vdash\top

always

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M},x\Vdash\bot

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad x=1

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M},x\Vdash p

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad x\in V(p)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M},x\Vdash\varphi\wedge\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad\mathfrak{M},x\Vdash\varphi\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},x\Vdash\psi

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M},x\Vdash\varphi\vee\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad\exists y,z\in X\text{ s.t. }\mathfrak{M},y\Vdash\varphi\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},z\Vdash\psi\text{ and }y\curlywedge z\preccurlyeq x

We write
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}:=\{x\in X\mid\mathfrak{M},x\Vdash\varphi\}

for the
truth set
of
\varphi
in
\mathfrak{M}
.
If the underlying (semi)lattice is fixed and we want to emphasise the role of the valuation
in the interpretation, we will write
V(\varphi)
instead of
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}
.
The
theory
of
x
is denoted by

\operatorname{th}_{\mathfrak{M}}(x):=\{\varphi\in\mathbf{L}\mid\mathfrak{M},x\Vdash\varphi\}
.

Note that the (semi)lattice underlying an L-model is uniquely determined
by its partial order. So we may view L-models as a type of relational
semantics, where the relation is used to define a non-standard interpretation
of joins.
When viewed as frame semantics, we denote the top element and meet of a
semilattice by
1
and
\curlywedge
and call the semilattice itself an L-frame.

0.3.8 Definition
.

We write
\mathfrak{M},x\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
if
x\in\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}

implies
x\in\llbracket\psi\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}
, and

\mathfrak{M}\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
if

\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}\subseteq\llbracket\psi\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}
.
If
\mathfrak{X}
is an L-frame, we let
\mathfrak{X},x\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
if

\mathfrak{M},x\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
for all L-models
\mathfrak{M}
based on
\mathfrak{X}
,
and
\mathfrak{X}\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
if
\mathfrak{X},x\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
for
all states
x
of
\mathfrak{X}
.

We say that
\mathfrak{M}
or
\mathfrak{X}

validates

\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi

if
\mathfrak{M}\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
or
\mathfrak{X}\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
, respectively.
If
\Gamma
is a set of consequence pairs, then we let

\mathsf{LFrm}(\Gamma)
denote the full subcategory of
\mathsf{LFrm}
whose
objects validate all consequence pairs in
\Gamma
.
We write
\varphi\Vdash_{\Gamma}\psi
if
\mathfrak{X}\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi

for all
\mathfrak{X}\in\mathsf{LFrm}(\Gamma)
.
If
\Gamma=\emptyset
then we write
\varphi\Vdash\psi
instead of

\varphi\Vdash_{\emptyset}\psi
.

For any L-frame
\mathfrak{F}=(X,\leq)
, the collection

\mathfrak{F}^{*}:=\mathcal{F}(X,\leq)
forms a lattice, called the
complex algebra

of
\mathfrak{F}
. Since valuations of
\mathfrak{F}
correspond bijectively to
assignments of
\mathfrak{F}^{*}
, we can define the
complex algebra

of an L-model
\mathfrak{M}=(X,\leq,V)
by

\mathfrak{M}^{*}=(\mathcal{F}(X,\leq),V)
.
A routine induction on the structure of
\varphi
then proves the following
lemma.

0.3.9 Lemma
.

For every L-model
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathbf{L}
-formula
\varphi
we have

\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}=\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\varphi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{\mathfrak{M}^{*}}
.

The next persistence result is similar to persistence in intuitionistic logic,
except we require formulae to be interpreted as (principal) filters rather than upsets.

0.3.10 Proposition

(Persistence)
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}=(X,1,\curlywedge,V)
be a (principal) L-model.
Then for each
\varphi\in\mathbf{L}
the truth set
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}

of
\varphi
is a (principal) filter of
(X,1,\curlywedge)
.

0.3.11 Proof
.

The fact that
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}
is a filter for each
\varphi

follows from Lemma
0.3.9
.
Suppose
\mathfrak{M}
is principal.
Then
\llbracket p\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}
is principal by definition,
as are
\llbracket\bot\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}={\uparrow}\top

and
\llbracket\top\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}={\uparrow}0
.
If
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}
or
\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}
then
we proceed by induction.
We may assume that

\llbracket\varphi_{1}\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}={\uparrow}x_{1}
and

\llbracket\varphi_{2}\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}={\uparrow}x_{2}
, so that

\llbracket\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}={\uparrow}(x_{1}\curlyvee x_{2})

and
\llbracket\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}={\uparrow}(x_{1}\curlywedge x_{2})
.

0.3.11 Theorem

(Soundness)
.

If
\varphi\vdash_{\Gamma}\psi
then
\varphi\Vdash_{\Gamma}\psi
.

0.3.12 Proof
.

If
\mathfrak{M}
is an L-model that validates all consequence pairs in
\Gamma
,
then
\mathfrak{M}^{*}\in\mathsf{Lat}(\Gamma)
.
Since
\varphi\vdash_{\Gamma}\psi
, Theorem
0.3.1
yields

\varphi\mathrel{\kern 0.43057pt\text{\hbox to7.06pt{\vbox to7.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.10971pt\lower-3.76735pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.92 L 0 4.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.55 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 -1.95 -1.67 -3.77 -0.08 -4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 1.95 -1.67 3.77 -0.08 4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}${}_{\Gamma}$}}\kern 0.43057pt\psi
, and hence

\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\varphi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{\mathfrak{M}^{*}}\leq\llparenthesis\kern 0.86108pt\psi\kern 0.86108pt\rrparenthesis_{\mathfrak{M}^{*}}
.
Lemma
0.3.9
now implies

\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}\leq\llbracket\psi\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}
,
so that
\mathfrak{M}
validates
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
.

We turn the collections of (principal) L-models into a category by
equipping with truth-preserving morphisms.

0.3.13 Definition
.

An
L-model morphism
from

(X,1,\curlywedge,V)
to
(X^{\prime},1^{\prime},\curlywedge^{\prime},V^{\prime})
is
an L-morphism (Definition
0.2.4
)

f:(X,1,\curlywedge)\to(X^{\prime},1^{\prime},\curlywedge^{\prime})

that satisfies
V=f^{-1}\circ V^{\prime}
.

A routine induction on the structure of
\varphi
shows that
L-model morphisms preserve and reflect truth of

\mathbf{L}
-formulae.

0.3.14 Proposition
.

Let
f:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
be an L-model morphism.
Then for all states
x
of
\mathfrak{M}
and all
\varphi\in\mathbf{L}
,

\mathfrak{M},x\Vdash\varphi\quad\text{iff}\quad\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},f(x)\Vdash\varphi.

The remainder of this subsection is devoted to examples of L-frames and -models.

0.3.15 Example
.

Any linearly ordered set with a largest element is an L-frame.
Filters in such frames are simply upsets.
For example
\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
with the usual ordering
is an L-frame which is prinicipal.
The set
\mathbb{N}
ordered by
\geq
is also an L-frame, with top
element
0
. It is not principal because it lacks a bottom element.

0.3.16 Example
.

As a special case of Example
0.3.15
,
consider the collection
\mathcal{P}_{\omega}X
of finite subsets of
X
.
This forms a semilattice with top element
\emptyset
,
and meet given by the set-theoretic union.
Filters of
\mathcal{P}_{\omega}X
correspond bijectively with subsets of
X
.

0.3.17 Example

(Propositional team semantics)
.

We briefly recall a simplified version of team semantics for propositional
logics, underlying versions of modal dependence and independence
logics such as the ones studied in
[
30
,
39
,
47
,
48
]
.
Let
\mathbf{T}(\Prop)
the language be given by the grammar

\varphi::=p\mid\neg p\mid\varphi\wedge\varphi\mid\varphi\vee\varphi
.
Then
\mathbf{T}(\Prop)
-formulae can be interpreted in models
consisting of a set
X
and a valuation

\Pi:\Prop\to\mathcal{P}X
of the proposition letters.
However, rather than assigning truth of formulae to elements of
X
,
truth is defined for
subsets
of
X
(the teams).
Let
\mathfrak{M}=(X,\Pi)
be such a model and
T\subseteq X
a team,
then we let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M},T\Vdash_{\!t}p

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad T\subseteq V(p)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M},T\Vdash_{\!t}\neg p

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad T\cap V(p)=\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M},T\Vdash_{\!t}\varphi\wedge\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad\mathfrak{M},T\Vdash_{\!t}\varphi\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},T\Vdash_{\!t}\psi

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M},T\Vdash_{\!t}\varphi\vee\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad\exists\,T_{1},T_{2}\subseteq T\text{ s.t. }T_{1}\cup T_{2}=T\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},T_{1}\Vdash_{\!t}\varphi\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},T_{2}\Vdash_{\!t}\psi

We can add
\top
, which is true for every team,
and
\bot
satisfying
\mathfrak{M},T\Vdash_{t}\bot
iff
T=\emptyset
.

This interpretation resembles
Definition
0.3.7
. Let us make this precise.
For a set
\Prop
of proposition letters, let

{\neg{\Prop}}=\{\neg p\mid p\in\Prop\}
.
Then, given a team model
\mathfrak{M}=(X,\Pi)
, we can define a principal L-model

\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{P}X,\emptyset,\cup,V)
, with

V(p)=\{a\in\mathcal{P}X\mid a\subseteq\Pi(p)\}

and
V(\neg p)=\{a\in\mathcal{P}X\mid a\cap\Pi(p)=\emptyset\}
.
Then for each team model
\mathfrak{M}
,
team
T
, and formula
\varphi\in\mathbf{T}(\Prop)
we have

\mathfrak{M},T\Vdash_{\!t}\varphi\quad\text{iff}\quad\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},T\Vdash\varphi.

This can be proven by induction on the structure of
\varphi
.
The only non-trivial step is for joins:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M},t\Vdash_{t}\varphi\vee\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad\exists T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathcal{P}X\text{ s.t. }T_{1}\cup T_{2}=T\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},T_{1}\Vdash_{t}\varphi\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},T_{2}\Vdash_{t}\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad\exists T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathcal{P}X\text{ s.t. }T_{1}\cup T_{2}=T\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},T_{1}\Vdash\varphi\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},T_{2}\Vdash\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad\exists T_{1}^{\prime},T_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}X\text{ s.t. }T_{1}^{\prime}\cup T_{2}^{\prime}\supseteq T\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},T_{1}^{\prime}\Vdash\varphi\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},T_{2}^{\prime}\Vdash\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad\mathfrak{M},T\Vdash\varphi\vee\psi

The first “iff” is the definition of
\Vdash_{t}
, the second follows from
the induction hypothesis. The third “iff” follows from persistence and the
fact that the frame is ordered by reverse inclusion, and the last “iff”
hold by the definition of
\Vdash
.

0.3.18 Example

(Modal information logic)
.

Modal information logic
[
3
]
is the extension of propositional classical
logic with two binary modal operators
\langle\mathsf{inf}\rangle
and
\langle\mathsf{sup}\rangle
.
These are interpreted in Kripke models
\mathfrak{M}=(X,R,V)
where

R
is a pre-order on
X
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M},x\Vdash\langle\mathsf{inf}\rangle(\varphi,\psi)

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad\exists y,z\in X\text{ s.t. }x=\inf(y,z)\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},y\Vdash\varphi\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},z\Vdash\psi

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M},x\Vdash\langle\mathsf{sup}\rangle(\varphi,\psi)

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad\exists y,z\in X\text{ s.t. }x=\sup(y,z)\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},y\Vdash\varphi\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},z\Vdash\psi

Note that we need not require that every pair of states has an infimum
and a supremum, nor that it is unique.
The definition simply uses the fact that they might
exist. Observe that we can recover the usual modal and temporal diamonds via

\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\varphi=\langle\mathsf{inf}\rangle(\varphi,\top)
and

\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\varphi=\langle\mathsf{sup}\rangle(\varphi,\top)
.

Clearly, every L-model is a model for modal information logic.
Interestingly, the interpretation of
\langle\mathsf{inf}\rangle
is closely aligned
to our interpretation of joins; the only difference is that the
infimum is allowed to be
below
the state under consideration.
Taking this into account, our interpretation of joins in an L-model

\mathfrak{M}=(X,1,\curlywedge,V)
coincides with

\varphi\vee\psi=\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt(\langle\mathsf{inf}\rangle(\varphi,\psi)),

where
\vee
is the non-classical join of weak positive logic.

0.3.3
Descriptive Frames and Completeness

We have already seen a duality for lattices by means of L-spaces.
Since every L-space is based on a complete semilattice,
L-spaces can be viewed as topologised (principal) L-frames.
In this subsection we define clopen valuations for L-spaces and
show how this gives rise to completeness results.
We denote L-spaces and L-spaces with a valuation by
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{M}
.
If
\mathbb{X}
is an L-space, then we write
\kappa\mathbb{X}
for its
underlying (principal) L-frame.

0.3.19 Definition
.

A
clopen valuation
for an L-space
\mathbb{X}
is an assignment

V:\Prop\to\mathcal{F}_{clp}\mathbb{X}
, which assigns to each proposition letter
a clopen filter of
\mathbb{X}
. We call a pair
\mathbb{M}=(\mathbb{X},V)

of an L-space and a clopen valuation an
L-space model
.
The interpretation
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathbb{M}}
of an
\mathbf{L}
-formula

\varphi
in an L-space model
\mathbb{M}=(\mathbb{X},V)
is defined as in
the underlying L-model
(\kappa\mathbb{X},V)
.

An L-space model
\mathbb{M}
validates a consequence pair
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi

if
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathbb{M}}\subseteq\llbracket\psi\rrbracket^{\mathbb{M}}
,
notation:
\mathbb{M}\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
.
We say that an L-space
\mathbb{X}
validates
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
if
every L-space model based on it validates
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
.
Finally, we write
\varphi\Vdash_{\mathsf{LSpace}}\psi
if every L-space
validates
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
.

0.3.20 Lemma
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be an L-space,
A
its dual lattice, and
\varphi,\psi\in\mathbf{L}
.
Then

\mathbb{X}\Vdash\varphi\text{ iff }A\mathrel{\kern 0.43057pt\text{\hbox to7.06pt{\vbox to7.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.10971pt\lower-3.76735pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.92 L 0 4.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.55 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 -1.95 -1.67 -3.77 -0.08 -4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 1.95 -1.67 3.77 -0.08 4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$}}\kern 0.43057pt\varphi\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{X}\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi\text{ iff }A\mathrel{\kern 0.43057pt\text{\hbox to7.06pt{\vbox to7.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.10971pt\lower-3.76735pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.92 L 0 4.92}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 6.55 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 -1.95 -1.67 -3.77 -0.08 -4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{{}{}{}{}}}{}
{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.62 0 C -2.62 1.95 -1.67 3.77 -0.08 4.89}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$}}\kern 0.43057pt\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi.

0.3.21 Proof
.

The first “iff” follows from the fact that clopen valuations of
\mathbb{X}

correspond bijectively to assignments of the proposition letters for
A
,
together with a routine induction on the structure of
\varphi
.
The second “iff” follows immediately from the first.

0.3.22 Remark
.

We can alternatively describe L-spaces as
descriptive L-frames
.
This is similar to the perspective of Esakia spaces as
descriptive intuitionistic Kripke frames, see
[
17
, Chapter 3]
and
[
8
, Section 8]
.
We briefly sketch this alternative perspective.

A
general L-frame
is a tuple
(X,1,\curlywedge,A)
such that

(X,1,\curlywedge)
is an L-frame and
A
is a collection of filters of

(X,1,\curlywedge)
containing
X
and
\emptyset
, and closed under

\cap
and
\mathbin{\triangledown}
.
Let
-A=\{X\setminus a\mid a\in A\}
.
A
descriptive L-frame
is a general L-frame
(X,1,\curlywedge,A)
that is

•

refined
: for all
x,y\in X
such that
x\not\preccurlyeq y

there exists an
a\in A
such that
x\in a
and
y\notin a
;

•

compact
: if
C\subseteq A\cup-A
has the finite
intersection property then
\bigcap C\neq\emptyset
.

A general L-morphism from
(X,1,\curlywedge,A)
to
(X^{\prime},1^{\prime},\curlywedge^{\prime},A^{\prime})
is an L-morphism

f:(X,1,\curlywedge)\to(X^{\prime},1^{\prime},\curlywedge^{\prime})
such that
f^{-1}(a^{\prime})\in A
for all
a^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}
.
Write
\mathsf{D\mathchar 45\relax LFrm}
for the category
of descriptive L-frames and general L-morphisms.
Then we have

\mathsf{D\mathchar 45\relax LFrm}\cong\mathsf{LSpace}.

Next, we use the notion of
\Pi_{1}
-preservation to prove a general
completeness result.

0.3.23 Definition
.

A consequence pair
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
is called
\Pi_{1}
-persistent
if
for every L-space
\mathbb{X}
,

\mathbb{X}\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi\quad\text{implies}\quad\kappa\mathbb{X}\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi.

It is well known that filter and ideal completions preserve all
(in)equalities (see e.g.
[
43
]
).
Combining this with Lemmas
0.3.9

and
0.3.20
we find:

0.3.24 Lemma
.

Any consequence pair
\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi
of
\mathbf{L}
-formulae is

\Pi_{1}
-persistent.

0.3.24 Theorem
.

Let
\Gamma
be a set of consequence pairs.
Then the logic
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}(\Gamma)
is sound and complete with respect to the
following classes of frames:

•

\mathsf{D\mathchar 45\relax LFrm}(\Gamma)

(
descriptive frames validating
\Gamma)
;

•

\mathsf{PLFrm}(\Gamma)

(
principal L-frames validating
\Gamma)
;

•

\mathsf{LFrm}(\Gamma)

(
L-frames validating
\Gamma)
.

0.3.25 Proof
.

Soundness holds by definition, so we prove completeness.
Suppose
\varphi\not\vdash_{\Gamma}\psi
.
Then by Theorem
0.3.1
we can find a lattice
A

validating all consequence pairs in
\Gamma
, but not
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
.
As a consequence of Lemma
0.3.20
the L-space

\mathbb{X}
dual to
A
validates all consequence pairs in
\Gamma

but does not validate
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
, thus we find completeness
with respect to
\mathsf{D\mathchar 45\relax LFrm}(\Gamma)
.

Since
\mathbb{X}\not\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi

there must exist a clopen valuation
V
such that

(\mathbb{X},V)\not\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
.
Therefore
(\kappa\mathbb{X},V)\not\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
.
Besides, Lemma
0.3.24
implies that

\kappa\mathbb{X}
validates all consequence pairs in
\Gamma
.
This implies completeness with respect to
\mathsf{LFrm}(\Gamma)
.
Lastly, we note that
\mathbb{X}
is a principal L-frame and
since
V
is clopen is assigns to each proposition letter a principal filter.
Thus
\mathbb{X}
is a principal L-frame validating
\Gamma
but not

\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
, proving completeness with respect to

\mathsf{PLFrm}(\Gamma)
.

0.3.26 Remark
.

Another way to prove Theorem
0.3.3
is via a Sahlqvist-style
argument and the correspondence proved in Section
0.3.4
.
This resembles the approach taken by Sambin and Vaccaro
[
45
]
.
For the detailed order-topological proof we refer to
[
14
, Section 4.2]
.
The basic idea is as follows: if a sequent is refuted on some
L
-frame,
then it is refuted on this frame via a minimal valuation which is closed.
An analogue of the so-called intersection lemma entails that the value of
a positive formula on a closed valuation is the intersection of the values
of this formula on a clopen valuation. This produces a clopen valuation
refuting the sequent.

0.3.27 Remark
.

The
\Pi_{1}
-persistence discussed here allows us to move from valuations that interpret proposition letters as clopen filters to valuations that assign to each proposition letter an arbitrary filter. It is analogous to
d
-persistence in intuitionistic and modal
logics
[
6
,
8
]

In the classical setting
d
-persistence allows one to move from clopen valuations to arbitrary valuations, and in the intuitionistic case from valuations into clopen upsets to valuations into
all upsets. We point out that, while in the distributive setting this corresponds algebraically to canonical extensions, in our setting the corresponding algebraic structure is the
\Pi_{1}
-completion.

0.3.4
The First-Order Translation and Sahlqvist Correspondence

In this section we define the standard translation of
\mathbf{L}
into a suitable
first-order logic. We use this to derive a Sahlqvist correspondence
result. We prove that for every consequence pair
\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi
,
the collection of L-frames validate
\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi
are first-order definable.
Our proof of the correspondence result follows a standard proof from normal
modal logic, such as found in
[
6
, Section 3.6]
.
Thus, it showcases how our duality for lattices allows us to
transfer classical techniques to the positive non-distributive setting.
However, it is complicated (or rather, made more interesting) by the
non-standard interpretation of disjunctions.

0.3.28 Definition
.

Let
\mathbf{FOL}
be the single-sorted first-order language which has a
unary predicate
P_{p}
for every proposition letter
p
,
and a binary relation symbol
R
.

Intuitively, the relation symbol of our first-order language accounts
of the poset structure of L-frames. It is used in the translation of
disjunctions.

If
x,y
and
z
are first-order variables, then we can express that

x
is above every lower bound of
y
and
z
in the ordering induced by the
relation symbol
R
using a first-order sentence.
In order to streamline notation we abbreviate this as follows:

\mathsf{abovemeet}(x;y,z):=\forall w((wRy\wedge wRz)\to wRx).

If
x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
is a finite set of variables and
n\geq 1
then we
define
\mathsf{abovemeet}(x;y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
in the obvious way.
In particular,
\mathsf{abovemeet}(x;y)
is simply
xRy
.

We are now ready to define the standard translation.

0.3.29 Definition
.

Let
x
be a first-order variable.
Define the standard translation
\st_{x}:\mathbf{L}\to\mathbf{FOL}
recursively
via

\displaystyle\st_{x}(p)

\displaystyle=P_{p}x

\displaystyle\st_{x}(\top)

\displaystyle=(x=x)

\displaystyle\st_{x}(\bot)

\displaystyle=\forall y(yRx)

\displaystyle\st_{x}(\varphi\wedge\psi)

\displaystyle=\st_{x}(\varphi)\wedge\st_{x}(\psi)

\displaystyle\st_{x}(\varphi\vee\psi)

\displaystyle=\exists y\exists z(\mathsf{abovemeet}(x;y,z)\wedge\st_{y}(\varphi)\wedge\st_{z}(\psi))

Furthermore, we define the standard translation of a consequence pair

\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
as

\displaystyle\st_{x}(\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi)

\displaystyle=\st_{x}(\varphi)\to\st_{x}(\psi).

Every L-model
\mathfrak{M}=(X,\leq,V)
gives rise to a first-order structure
for
\mathbf{FOL}
:
\leq
accounts for the interpretation of the
binary relation symbol, and the interpretation of the unary
predicates is given via the valuations of the proposition letters.
We write
\mathfrak{M}^{\circ}
for the L-model
\mathfrak{M}
conceived of as
a first-order structure for
\mathbf{FOL}
.

0.3.30 Proposition
.

For every L-model
\mathfrak{M}
and state
w
of
\mathfrak{M}
we have

1.

\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\varphi
iff
\mathfrak{M}^{\circ}\models\st_{x}(\varphi)[w]
;

2.

\mathfrak{M},w\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi

iff
\mathfrak{M}^{\circ}\models\st_{x}(\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi)[w]
.

0.3.31 Proof
.

The first item follows immediately from the definition of the standard
translation, and the other item follows from the first one.

In order to obtain similar results as in Proposition
0.3.30

for frames, we need to quantify the unary predicates in
\mathbf{FOL}
corresponding
to the proposition letters. We can do so in a second-order language, say,

\mathbf{SOL}
. However, getting a second-order correspondent for a consequence
pair
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
that is satisfied in a frame if and only if

\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
is, is not as easy as simply quantifying over all
possible interpretations of the unary predicates.
That is, we cannot simply add
\forall P_{1}\cdots\forall P_{n}
in front
of
\st_{x}(\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi)
. Indeed, we wish to only take those interpretations
into account that arise from a valuation of the proposition letters

as filters
.

Thus we wish to quantify over interpretations of the unary predicates
corresponding to filters in the underlying frame.
We can force this by adding conditions that ensure that the
P
’s are
interpreted as filters in the antecedent of the implication

\st_{x}(\varphi)\to\st_{x}(\psi)
. Then the implication is vacuously true
for “illegal” interpretations of the unary predicates.
This intuition motivates the following definition of the

second-order translation
of a consequence pair.

0.3.32 Definition
.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
be the proposition letters occurring in
\psi

and
\chi
, and let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
denote their corresponding unary
predicates.
For each
P_{i}
, abbreviate

\mathsf{isfil}(P_{i})=\exists wP_{i}w\wedge\forall x\forall y\forall z((P_{i}y\wedge P_{i}z\wedge\mathsf{abovemeet}(x;y,z))\to P_{i}x).

Using this abbreviation, we define the
second order translation

of a consequence pair
\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi
by

\so(\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi)=\forall P_{1}\cdots\forall P_{n}\big((\mathsf{isfil}(P_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathsf{isfil}(P_{n})\wedge\st_{x}(\psi))\to\st_{x}(\chi)\big).

(3)

To disburden notation, we will often abbreviate

\mathsf{isfil}(P_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathsf{isfil}(P_{n})
as
\operatorname{ISFIL}
.

Since all unary predicates in
\so(\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi)
are in the scope of a quantifier,
the formula
\so(\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi)
can be interpreted in a first-order structure with a single relation.
Therefore, every L-model
\mathfrak{X}
gives rise to a structure

\mathfrak{X}^{\circ}
for
\mathbf{SOL}
in which we can interpret second order
translations.

0.3.33 Lemma
.

For all L-frames
\mathfrak{X}=(X,1,\curlywedge)
and all consequence pairs

\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi
we have

\mathfrak{X},w\Vdash\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi\quad\text{iff}\quad\mathfrak{X}^{\circ}\models\so(\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi)[w].

0.3.34 Proof
.

If
\mathfrak{X},w\Vdash\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi
then
w\in V(\psi)

implies
w\in V(\chi)

for every valuation
V
for
\mathfrak{X}
.
If any of the
P_{i}
is interpreted as a subset of
X
that is not
a filter, then the implication inside the quantifiers in (
3
)
is automatically true, because the
antecedent is false. If all
P_{i}
are interpreted as filters,
then the implication holds because of the assumption.
The converse is similar.

Next, we show how one can use the second-order translation to obtain
local correspondence results.
We first define what we mean by local correspondence.

0.3.35 Definition
.

Let
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
be a consequence pair and
\alpha(x)
a first-order
formula with free variable
x
. Then we say that
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
and

\alpha(x)
are
local frame correspondents
if for any L-frame

\mathfrak{X}
and any state
w
we have

\mathfrak{X},w\Vdash\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi\quad\text{iff}\quad\mathfrak{X}\models\alpha(x)[w].

Since our language is positive, every formula is upward monotone.
That is, extending the valuation increases the truth set of formulae.

0.3.36 Lemma
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}
be an L-frame and let
V
and
V^{\prime}
be valuations for
\mathfrak{X}

such that
V(p)\subseteq V^{\prime}(p)
for all
p\in\Prop
.
Then for all
\varphi\in\mathbf{L}
we have
V(\varphi)\subseteq V^{\prime}(\varphi)
.

We now prove that every consequence pair has a local correspondent.

0.3.36 Theorem
.

Any consequence pair
\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi
of
\mathbf{L}
-formulae
locally corresponds to a first-order formula with one free variable.

0.3.37 Proof
.

We know that
\mathfrak{X},w\Vdash\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi
if and only if

\mathfrak{X}^{\circ}\models\so(\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi)[w]
.
Our strategy for obtaining a first-order correspondent is to remove all
second-order quantifiers from the second-order translation.
We assume that no two quantifiers bind the same variable.

If
\psi
is equivalent to
\top
then as a consequence of Lemma
0.3.36

\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi
is equivalent
to
\top\trianglelefteqslant\chi^{\prime}
, where
\chi^{\prime}
is obtained
from
\chi
by replacing all proposition letters with
\bot
.
This, in turn, implies that
\so(\top\trianglelefteqslant\chi^{\prime})
is a first-order
correspondent of
\top\trianglelefteqslant\chi
, since the lack of proposition
letters in
\chi^{\prime}
implies that there are no second-order
quantifiers in
\so(\top\trianglelefteqslant\chi^{\prime})
.
If
\psi
is equivalent to
\bot
then
\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi
is vacuously valid on all L-frames.
So we may assume that the antecedent does not use
\top
or
\bot
.

Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
be the propositional variables occurring in

\psi
, and write
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
for their corresponding unary predicates.
We assume that every proposition letter that occurs in
\chi

also occurs is
\psi
, for otherwise we may replace it by
\bot
to
obtain a formula which is equivalent in terms of validity on frames.

Step 1.
Use equivalences of the form

(\exists w(\alpha(w))\wedge\beta)\leftrightarrow\exists w(\alpha(w)\wedge\beta),\qquad(\exists w(\alpha(w))\to\beta)\leftrightarrow\forall w(\alpha(w)\to\beta)

to pull out all quantifiers that arise in
\st_{x}(\psi)
.
Let
Y:=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\}
denote the set of (bound) variables that occur
in the antecedent of the implication from the second-order translation.
We end up with a formula of the form

\forall P_{1}\cdots\forall P_{n}\forall y_{1}\cdots\forall y_{m}\big((\operatorname{ISFIL}\wedge\operatorname{AT}\wedge\operatorname{REL})\to\st_{x}(\chi)\big).

(4)

Here
\operatorname{ISFIL}=\mathsf{isfil}(P_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathsf{isfil}(P_{n})
,

\operatorname{AT}
is a conjunction of formulae of the form
P_{i}z
and

\operatorname{REL}
is a conjunction of formulae of the form
\mathsf{abovemeet}(z;z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime})
,
where
z,z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime}\in Y\cup\{x\}
.

Step 2. Next we read off minimal instances of the
P_{i}
making the
antecedent true. Intuitively, these correspond to the smallest valuations
for the
p_{i}
making the antecedent true.
For each proposition letter
P_{i}
, let
P_{i}y_{i_{1}},\ldots,P_{i}y_{i_{k}}
be
the occurrences of
P_{i}
in
\operatorname{AT}
in the antecedent of (
4
).
We define the valuation of
p_{i}
to be the filter generated
by the (interpretations of)
y_{i_{1}},\ldots,y_{i_{k}}
.
Formally,

\sigma(P_{i}):=\lambda u.\mathsf{abovemeet}(u;y_{i_{1}},\ldots,y_{i_{k}}).

(If
k=0
, i.e. there is no variable
y
with
P_{i}y
, then we let

\sigma(P_{i})=\lambda u.(u\neq u)
.)
Then for each L-model
\mathfrak{M}
and states
x^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,y_{m}^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{M}

we have

\mathfrak{M}^{\circ}\models\operatorname{AT}\wedge\operatorname{REL}[x,y_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,y_{m}^{\prime}]\quad\text{implies}\quad\mathfrak{M}^{\circ}\models\forall y(\sigma(P_{i})y\to P_{i}y).

If we replace each unary predicate
P
in (
4
) with

\sigma(P)
, then all conjoints in
\operatorname{ISFIL}
and
\operatorname{AT}
become true.
Writing
[\sigma(P)/P]\st_{x}(\chi)
for the formula obtained from
\st_{x}(\chi)

by replacing each instance of a unary predicate
P
with
\sigma(P)
,
we arrive at the first-order formula

\forall y_{1}\cdots\forall y_{m}\big(\operatorname{REL}\to[\sigma(P_{i})/P_{i}]\st_{x}(\chi)\big)

(5)

Step 3.
Finally, we claim that for every L-frame
\mathfrak{X}
,

\mathfrak{X}^{\circ}
validates (
4
) if and only if
it validates (
5
).
The implication from left to right is simply an instantiation of
the quantifiers as filters.
For the converse, assume that
\mathfrak{M}
is some model based on
\mathfrak{X}
,
so that
\mathfrak{M}^{\circ}
is an extension of
\mathfrak{X}^{\circ}
giving
the interpretations of the unary predicates as filters. We may disregard
the case where any of them is not a filter as that would make the antecedent
in (
4
) false, hence the whole implication true.
Let
x^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,y_{m}^{\prime}
be states in
\mathfrak{M}

and assume that

\mathfrak{M}^{\circ}\models\operatorname{ISFIL}\wedge\operatorname{AT}\wedge\operatorname{REL}[x^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,y_{m}^{\prime}].

(6)

We need to show that
\mathfrak{M}^{\circ}\models\st_{x}(\chi)[x^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,y_{m}^{\prime}]
.
It follows from the assumption that (
5
) holds that

\mathfrak{M}^{\circ}\models[\sigma(P)/P]\st_{x}(\chi)[x^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,y_{m}^{\prime}]
.
Moreover, as a consequence of (
6
) we have

\mathfrak{M}^{\circ}\models\forall y(\sigma(P)(y)\to Py)
for all

P\in\{P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}\}
.
Using Lemma
0.3.36
it follows that

\mathfrak{M}^{\circ}\models\st_{x}(\chi)[x^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,y_{m}^{\prime}]
, as desired.

Let us work through some explicit examples so we can see the proof of
the theorem in action.
Recall that
\mathsf{abovemeet}(x;y)
is simply
xRy
.

0.3.38 Example
.

Consider the formula
p\wedge(q\vee q^{\prime})\trianglelefteqslant(p\wedge q)\vee(p\wedge q^{\prime})
.
This corresponds to distributivity; the reverse consequence pair is always valid.
We temporarily abbreviate
\chi:=(p\wedge q)\vee(p\wedge q^{\prime})
.
The second-order translation of this formula is

\displaystyle\so({p\wedge(q\vee q^{\prime})}\trianglelefteqslant\chi)

\displaystyle=\forall P\forall Q\forall Q^{\prime}\big(\big[\mathsf{isfil}(P)\wedge\mathsf{isfil}(Q)\wedge\mathsf{isfil}(Q^{\prime})

\displaystyle\wedge Px\wedge\exists y\exists y^{\prime}(\mathsf{abovemeet}(x;y,y^{\prime})\wedge Qy\wedge Q^{\prime}y^{\prime})\big]\to\st_{x}(\chi)\big)

As per instructions, we rewrite this to

\forall P\forall Q\forall Q^{\prime}\forall y\forall y^{\prime}\big((\operatorname{ISFIL}\wedge Px\wedge\mathsf{abovemeet}(x;y,y^{\prime})\wedge Qy\wedge Q^{\prime}y^{\prime})\to\st_{x}(\chi)\big).

(7)

Next, we obtain

\sigma(P)=\lambda u.\mathsf{abovemeet}(u;x)
, which is simply
xRu
.
Similarly we find
\sigma(Q)=\lambda u.yRu

and
\sigma(Q^{\prime})=\lambda u.y^{\prime}Ru
.
Plugging these into the antecedent yields

[\sigma(P_{i})/P_{i}](\st_{x}(\chi))=\exists z\exists z^{\prime}\big(\mathsf{abovemeet}(x;z,z^{\prime})\wedge(xRz)\wedge(yRz)\wedge(xRz^{\prime})\wedge(y^{\prime}Rz^{\prime})\big).

Thus we find the following first-order correspondent:

\displaystyle\forall x\forall y\forall y^{\prime}\big(\mathsf{abovemeet}(x;y,y^{\prime})\to\big[

\displaystyle\exists z\exists z^{\prime}(\mathsf{abovemeet}(x;z,z^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip-25.00003pt\wedge(xRz)\wedge(yRz)

\displaystyle\hskip-25.00003pt\wedge(xRz^{\prime})\wedge(y^{\prime}Rz^{\prime}))\big]\big).

Recall that the predicate
R
is interpreted as the partial order
\preccurlyeq

underlying an L-frame.
Furthermore, since
z\wedge z^{\prime}Rx
and
xRz
and
xRz^{\prime}
,
we find that
z\wedge z^{\prime}=x
.
Thus, a state
w
in an L-frame
(X,1,\curlywedge)
with partial order
\preccurlyeq
,
satisfies distributivity if and only if

\forall y\forall y^{\prime}\big((y\curlywedge y^{\prime}\preccurlyeq w)\to\exists z\exists z^{\prime}((w=z\curlywedge z^{\prime})\wedge(y\preccurlyeq z)\wedge(y^{\prime}\preccurlyeq z^{\prime}))\big).

Therefore, an L-frame validates distributivity if it is a distributive semilattice.
Note that here
\curlywedge
and
\preccurlyeq
are L-frame operators while
the quantifiers and
\to,\wedge
are connectives from the first-order
language used to reason about L-frames.

0.3.39 Example
.

Next consider the modularity axiom

((p_{1}\wedge p_{3})\vee p_{2})\wedge p_{3}\trianglelefteqslant(p_{1}\wedge p_{3})\vee(p_{2}\wedge p_{3}).

Writing
\chi
for the right hand side of the consequence pair,
after applying Step 1 of the proof of Theorem
0.3.4
we have

\forall P_{1}\forall P_{2}\forall P_{3}\forall y\forall y^{\prime}\big((\operatorname{ISFIL}\wedge P_{1}y\wedge P_{3}y\wedge P_{2}y^{\prime}\wedge P_{3}x\wedge\mathsf{abovemeet}(x;y,y^{\prime}))\to\st_{x}(\chi)\big)

(8)

We then get
\sigma(P_{1})=\lambda u.yRu
,

\sigma(P_{2})=\lambda u.y^{\prime}Ru
and

\sigma(P_{3})=\lambda u.\mathsf{abovemeet}(u;x,y)
.
Substituting these and leaving out
\operatorname{ISFIL}
and
\operatorname{AT}
yields

\begin{split}&\forall y\forall y^{\prime}\big[\big(\mathsf{abovemeet}(x;y,y^{\prime})\wedge\mathsf{abovemeet}(y;x,y)\wedge\mathsf{abovemeet}(x;x,y)\big)\\
&\to\exists z\exists z^{\prime}(\mathsf{abovemeet}(x;z,z^{\prime})\wedge(yRz)\wedge\mathsf{abovemeet}(z;x,y)\wedge(y^{\prime}Rz^{\prime})\wedge\mathsf{abovemeet}(z^{\prime};x,y))\big].\end{split}

Leaving out everything that is trivially true, this yields the following
condition. A world
w
in an L-frame
(X,1,\curlywedge)
with partial
order
\preccurlyeq
satisfies the modularity axiom if and only if

\forall y\forall y^{\prime}((y\curlywedge y^{\prime}\preccurlyeq w)\to\exists z\exists z^{\prime}((z\curlywedge z^{\prime}\preccurlyeq w)\wedge(y\preccurlyeq z)\wedge(y^{\prime}\preccurlyeq z^{\prime})\wedge(w\curlywedge y\preccurlyeq z^{\prime})).

In yet other words,

w
satisfies modularity if for all
y,y^{\prime}\in X
such that

y\curlywedge y^{\prime}\preccurlyeq w
we can find

z,z^{\prime}
above
y,y^{\prime}
, respectively, such that

z\curlywedge z^{\prime}\preccurlyeq w
and
w\curlywedge y\preccurlyeq z^{\prime}
.

0.4
Normal Modal Extension

We investigate the extension of weak positive logic with two
modal operators,
and
, interpreted via a relation in the
usual way (see e.g.
[
6
, Definition 1.20]
).
As our point of departure we take L-frames with an additional relation.
We stipulate sufficient conditions on the relation to ensure persistence,
but we do not enforce any axioms on the modalities.
It then turns out that
preserves finite conjunctions,
while, as a consequence of the non-standard interpretation of disjunctions,

only satisfies monotonicity.
This is reminiscent of the modal extension of intuitionistic logic investigated
by Kojima
[
38
]
.
The interaction axioms relating
and
are closely aligned
to Dunn’s axioms for positive modal logic
[
15
]
,
see Remark
0.4.11
.

After investigating the modal logic from a semantic point of view, we use
our newly developed intuition to give syntactic definition of the logic
and its algebraic semantics in Section
0.4.2
,
and a duality in Section
0.4.3
.
We then extend the definition of the filter- and
\Pi_{1}
-completion
to the modal setting and prove completeness for weak positive modal logic
in Section
0.4.4
.

Finally, in Section
0.4.5
we extend the Sahlqvist
correspondence result for weak positive logic to the modal setting.
It is no longer the case that any consequence pair is Sahlqvist,
and we identify as Sahlqvist consequence pairs
precisely the negation-free Sahlqvist formulae from normal modal logic

[
6
, Definition 3.51]
, where the implication is replaced by
\trianglelefteqslant
.

One may wonder whether it would be more natural to insist that

be normal as well. We do not because the additional conditions required
to ensure that
is normal complicate the presentation of the
semantics and duality. Moreover, in order to make
normal we
only need to extend our basic system with the consequence pair

\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

.
We derive an appropriate notion of modal L-frame, such that the truth set of
each formula is guaranteed to be a filter.

0.4.1 Definition
.

A
modal L-frame
is a tuple
(X,1,\curlywedge,R)
where

(X,1,\curlywedge)
is an L-frame with underlying partial order
\preccurlyeq
,
and
R
is a binary relation on
X
such that:

1.

If
x\preccurlyeq y
and
yRz
then there exists a
w\in X
such that

xRw
and
w\preccurlyeq y
;

2.

If
x\preccurlyeq y
and
xRw
then there exists a
z\in X
such that

yRz
and
w\preccurlyeq z
;

3.

If
(x\curlywedge y)Rz
then there exist
v,w\in X
such that

xRv
and
yRw
and
v\curlywedge w\preccurlyeq z
;

4.

If
xRv
and
yRw
then
(x\curlywedge y)R(v\curlywedge w)
;

5.

For all
x
,
1Rx
if and only if
x=1
.

A
modal L-model
is a a modal L-frame together with a valuation
V

that assigns to each proposition letter a filter of
(X,1,\curlywedge)
.

Just like in the propositional case in Section
0.3.2
,
we can identify a class of frames where formulae can be interpreted
exclusively as
principal
filters.
To this end, we define a
principal modal L-frame
as a
modal L-frame
(X,1,\curlywedge,R)
that additionally satisfies:

0.

(X,1,\curlywedge)
has binary joins and all non-empty meets;

3^{\prime}
.

If
(\bigcurlywedge x_{i})Rz
, where the
i
range over some index set
I
,
then there exist
z_{i}
such that
x_{i}Rz_{i}
for all
i\in I

and
\bigcurlywedge z_{i}\leq z
;

4^{\prime}
.

If
x_{i}Ry_{i}
, where
i
ranges over some index set
I
,
then
(\bigcurlywedge x_{i})R(\bigcurlywedge y_{i})
;

Clearly, items (

3
′

) and (

4
′

)
subsume (
3
) and (
4
).
A principal modal L-model is a principal modal L-frame with a valuation
that assigns to each proposition letter a principal filter.

x
y
z
w
R
R
(
1
)

x
y
z
w
R
R
(
2
)

x
y
x\curlywedge y
z
v
w
v\curlywedge w
R
R
R
(
3
)

x
y
x\curlywedge y
v
w
v\curlywedge w
R
R
R
(
4
)

Figure 2:
The four conditions of a modal L-frame.
Lines denote the poset order, with high nodes being bigger.
Arrows denote the relation
R
.

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad\forall y\in X,xRy\text{ implies }\mathfrak{M},y\Vdash\varphi

\displaystyle\mathfrak{M},x\Vdash\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\varphi

\displaystyle\quad\text{iff}\quad\exists y\in X\text{ such that }xRy\text{ and }\mathfrak{M},y\Vdash\varphi

Satisfaction and validity of formulae and modal consequence pairs
are defined as expected.
In particular, if
{K}
is a class of modal L-frames and
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi

is a modal consequence pair, then we write

\varphi\Vdash_{{K}}\psi
if the consequence pair
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
is valid
on all frames in
{K}
.

The first four conditions of a modal L-frame are depicted in
Figure
2
.
Observe that (
1
) and (
5
) together imply
seriality, i.e. every state has an
R
-successor.
If
(X,1,\curlywedge,R)
is a modal L-frame, then we define
for
x\in X
and filters
a\subseteq X
:

R[x]:=\{y\in X\mid xRy\},\qquad[R]a=\{x\in X\mid R[x]\subseteq a\},\qquad\langle R\rangle a=\{x\in X\mid R[x]\cap a\neq\emptyset\}.

Definition
0.4.1
(
1
) and (
2
)
together say that
x\leq y
implies
R[x]\sqsubseteq R[y]
, where

\sqsubseteq
denotes the Egli-Milner order on

\mathcal{P}X

[
1
, Definition 6.2.2]
.
Furthermore, if
\mathfrak{M}
is a modal L-model then

\llbracket\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.68pt{\vbox to5.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84166pt\lower-2.84166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}=[R]\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}},\qquad\llbracket\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}=\langle R\rangle\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}.

Next we prove persistence.

0.4.3 Proposition
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}=(X,1,\curlywedge,R,V)
be a (principal) modal L-model.
Then for each

the set

.

.

We assume that
\mathfrak{M}
is not principal; the case for principal modal
L-models is similar.
The proof proceeds by induction on the structure of
\varphi
.
The only non-trivial cases are the modal cases.

;

.

.

) implies
\mathfrak{M},1\Vdash\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\psi
.
We conclude that
\llbracket\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\psi\rrbracket^{\mathfrak{M}}
is a filter
in
(X,1,\curlywedge)
.

0.4.5 Remark
.

We could have slightly weakened condition
4
by requiring
the existence of some
(x\curlywedge y)
-successor above
v\curlywedge w
.
We use the current formulation because it aligns more closely
to the notion of a modal L-space.

Morphisms between modal L-frames and -models are a combination of
L-morphisms and an adaptation of p-morphisms for positive modal
logic
[
7
]
.

0.4.6 Definition
.

A
bounded L-morphism
from
(X,1,\curlywedge,R)
to

(X^{\prime},1^{\prime},\curlywedge^{\prime},R^{\prime})
is an L-morphism

f:(X,1,\curlywedge)\to(X^{\prime},1^{\prime},\curlywedge^{\prime})

such that for all
x,y\in X
and
z^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
:

1.

If
xRy
then
f(x)R^{\prime}f(y)
;

2.

If
f(x)R^{\prime}z^{\prime}
then there exists a
z\in X
such that

xRz
and
f(z)\preccurlyeq z^{\prime}
;

3.

If
f(x)R^{\prime}z^{\prime}
then there exists a
w\in X
such that

xRw
and
z^{\prime}\preccurlyeq^{\prime}f(w)
.

(See also Figure
3
.)
A bounded L-morphism between models is bounded L-morphism between
the underlying frames that preserves and reflects truth of proposition letters.

x
y
f(x)
f(y)
R
f
f
R
(
1
)

x
z
f(x)
f(z)
z^{\prime}
R
f
f
R
\preccurlyeq
(
2
)

x
w
f(x)
f(w)
z^{\prime}
R
f
f
R
\preccurlyeq
(
3
)

Figure 3:
The conditions of a bounded L-morphism.

.

Let
\mathfrak{M}=(X,1,\curlywedge,R,V)
and
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}=(X^{\prime},1^{\prime},\curlywedge^{\prime},R^{\prime},V^{\prime})
be
two (principal) modal L-models. If
f:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
is a bounded L-morphism,

x\in X
and
\varphi\in\mathbf{L}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\text{\hbox to3.98pt{\vbox to3.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.98917pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

.

We give a number of modal consequence pairs that are valid in
every modal L-frame. These motivate the definition of a modal lattice
in Section
0.4.2
.

0.4.9 Lemma
.

Let
(X,1,\curlywedge,R)
be a modal L-frame. The following consequence
pairs are valid:

(normality and duality)

0.4.10 Proof
.

All of these follow immediately from the definition of the interpretation
of
and
.
In particular, they do not rely on any of the conditions from
Definition
0.4.1
.

Observe that the consequence pair
\top\trianglelefteqslant\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\top
corresponds
to seriality, i.e. the frame condition that every state has an
R
-successor.
In presence of

If
\Gamma
is a set of modal consequence pairs then

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{L}}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\text{\hbox to3.98pt{\vbox to3.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.98917pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.60275pt\!\hskip 0.60275pt\text{\hbox to4.62pt{\vbox to4.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.31197pt\lower-2.31197pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.60275pt}(\Gamma)
denotes the smallest set of modal consequence pairs
closed under the axioms and rules above and those in
\Gamma
.
We write
\varphi\vdash_{\Gamma}\psi
if

and
\varphi\dashv\vdash_{\Gamma}\psi
if both
\varphi\vdash_{\Gamma}\psi

and
\psi\vdash_{\Gamma}\varphi
,
omitting
\Gamma
if it is empty.

Observe that Becker’s rule together with linearity for

implies that
is a normal modal operator.
The algebraic semantics of the logic is given by modal lattices.

.
We write
\mathsf{MLat}
for the category of modal lattice and modal lattice
homomorphisms.

0.4.14 Example
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}=(X,1,\curlywedge,R)
be a modal L-frame.
Then
\mathfrak{X}^{*}:=(\mathcal{F}(X,1,\curlywedge),[R],\langle R\rangle)
is a modal lattice.
If
\mathfrak{X}
is principal then

\mathfrak{X}^{\ddagger}:=(\mathcal{F}_{p}(X,1,\curlywedge),[R],\langle R\rangle)
is
a modal lattice.

Formulae
\varphi\in\mathbf{L}_{\hskip 0.60275pt\text{\hbox to3.98pt{\vbox to3.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\thinspace\lower-1.98917pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

}

can be interpreted in
