}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt\cdots\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.68pt{\vbox to5.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84166pt\lower-2.84166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt}_{\text{$n$ times}}p,

where
p
is a proposition letter.
A
Sahlqvist antecedent
is a formula made from boxed atoms,

\top
and
\bot
by freely using
\wedge
,
\vee
and
.
A modal consequence pair
\varphi\trianglelefteqslant\psi
is called
Sahlqvist
if

\varphi
is a Sahlqvist antecedent (and
\psi
is any formula).

If
R
is a relation, then we write
R^{n}
for the
n
-fold composition
of
R
. That is,
xR^{n}y
if there exist
x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}
such that

x=x_{0}
,
y=x_{n}
and
x_{i}Rx_{i+1}
for all
i\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
.
With this definition, truth of
\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.68pt{\vbox to5.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84166pt\lower-2.84166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt^{n}p
in a modal L-model

\mathfrak{M}=(X,\leq,R,V)
can be given as

\mathfrak{M},x\Vdash\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.68pt{\vbox to5.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84166pt\lower-2.84166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt^{n}p\quad\text{iff}\quad\forall y\in X,xR^{n}y\text{ implies }\mathfrak{M},y\Vdash p.

We legislate
xR^{0}y
iff
x=y
. Then the interpretation of
\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.68pt{\vbox to5.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84166pt\lower-2.84166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108pt^{0}p

simply coincides with
p
.

0.4.49 Theorem
.

Any Sahlqvist modal consequence pair
\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi
locally corresponds to
a first-order formula with one free variable.

0.4.50 Proof
.

By Lemma … we have
\mathfrak{X},w\Vdash\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi
if and only if

\mathfrak{X}^{\circ}\models\so(\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi)[w]
.
As in Theorem
0.3.4
, our strategy for obtaining a first-order
formula is to remove all second-order quantifiers from
\so(\psi\trianglelefteqslant\chi)[w]
.
We assume that no two quantifiers bind the same variable.
The case where
\psi=\top
or
\bot
can be handled as in
Theorem
0.3.4
.
Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
be the propositional variables occurring in

\psi
, and write
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
for their corresponding unary predicates.
We assume that every proposition letter that occurs in
\chi

also occurs is
\psi
, for otherwise we may replace it by
\bot
to
obtain a formula which is equivalent in terms of validity on frames.

Step 1.
Use equivalences of the form

(\exists w(\alpha(w))\wedge\beta)\leftrightarrow\exists w(\alpha(w)\wedge\beta),\qquad(\exists w(\alpha(w))\vee\beta)\leftrightarrow\exists w(\alpha(w)\vee\beta),

and

(\exists w(\alpha(w))\to\beta)\leftrightarrow\forall w(\alpha(w)\to\beta)

to pull out all existential quantifiers that arise in
\st_{x}(\psi)
.
Let
Y:=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\}
denote the set of (bound) variables that arise
in the antecedent of the implication from the second-order translation.
We end up with a formula of the form

\forall P_{1}\cdots\forall P_{n}\forall y_{1}\cdots\forall y_{m}\big((\operatorname{ISFIL}\wedge\operatorname{AT}\wedge\operatorname{REL})\to\st_{x}(\chi)\big)

where

•

\operatorname{ISFIL}
is a conjunction of formulae of the form
\mathsf{isfil}(P_{i})
;

•

\operatorname{BOX\mathchar 45\relax AT}
is a conjunction of standard translations of boxed proposition letters,
i.e. formulae of the form
\forall z(z^{\prime}R^{n}z\to P_{i}z)

(here
P_{i}z
is viewed as
\forall z(z^{\prime}R^{0}z\to P_{i}z)
);

•

\operatorname{REL}
is a conjunction of formulae of the form
zRz^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{abovemeet}(z;,z^{\prime},z^{\prime\prime})
.

Step 2. Next we read off minimal instances of the
P_{i}
making the
antecedent true.
For each proposition letter
P_{i}
, let
\forall y_{i_{1}}(z_{i_{1}}R^{n_{1}}y_{i_{1}}\to P_{i}y_{i_{1}}),\ldots,\forall y_{i_{k}}(z_{i_{k}}R^{n_{k}}y_{i_{k}}\to P_{i}y_{i_{k}})
be
the occurrences of
P_{i}
in
\operatorname{BOX\mathchar 45\relax AT}
in the antecedent of (
).
Intuitively, we define the valuation of
p_{i}
to be the filter generated
by the (interpretations of)
y_{i_{1}},\ldots,y_{i_{k}}
.
Formally,

\displaystyle\sigma(P_{i}):=\bigvee\Big\{

\displaystyle\exists w_{i_{1}}\cdots\exists w_{i_{k}}\big(z_{i_{1}}R^{n_{1}}w_{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge z_{i_{k}}R^{n_{k}}w_{i_{k}}

\displaystyle\wedge\mathsf{abovemeet}(u;w_{i_{1}},\ldots,w_{i_{k}})\big)\mid\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}\Big\}.

(If
k=0
, i.e. there are no boxed atoms involving
P_{i}
in the formula,
then we take the empty meet to be falsum, i.e.
\sigma(P_{i})=\lambda u.(u\neq u)
.)

The remainder of the proof is analogous to the proof of
Theorem
0.3.4
.

In the next example we apply the algorithm of the proof of
Theorem
0.4.6
to two simple consequence pairs,

p\trianglelefteqslant\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptp
and
\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.68pt{\vbox to5.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84166pt\lower-2.84166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptp\trianglelefteqslant p
.
The shows the mechanism of the proof in action.
Moreover, it demonstrates that the duality between
and

is weaker than in the classical case, because the formulae
locally correspond to different frame conditions.

In the correspondents, we write
R
for the modal relation and
\preccurlyeq

for the poset order. Technically this should be
S
, which is then
interpreted as
\preccurlyeq
.
Besides, note that
\mathsf{abovemeet}(u;z)
is the same as
zSu
, which
we denote by
z\preccurlyeq u
.
Similarly,
\mathsf{abovemeet}(u;z,z^{\prime})
means
z\curlywedge z^{\prime}\preccurlyeq u
.

0.4.51 Example
.

The second-order translation of
p\trianglelefteqslant\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptp
is

\forall P(\mathsf{isfil}(P)\wedge Px\to\exists y(xRy\wedge Py))

This is already of the desired shape, so we proceed to Step 2.
We find
\sigma(P)=\lambda u.x\preccurlyeq u
. Substituting this gives
the first-order formula

\forall x(\mathsf{isfil}(P)\wedge(x\preccurlyeq x)\to\exists y(xRy\wedge(x\preccurlyeq y)))
.
The antecedent of the formula is always true, so the
(simplified) local correspondent of
p\trianglelefteqslant\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptp
is

\exists y(xRy\wedge x\preccurlyeq y).

Thus, a frame satisfies
p\trianglelefteqslant\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -4.21 M -0.04 -4.17 L -4.17 -0.04 C -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 0.02 -4.17 0.04 L -0.04 4.17 C -0.02 4.19 0.02 4.19 0.04 4.17 L 4.17 0.04 C 4.19 0.02 4.19 -0.02 4.17 -0.04 L 0.04 -4.17 C 0.02 -4.19 -0.02 -4.19 -0.04 -4.17 Z M 0 4.21}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptp
if

\forall x\exists y(xRy\wedge x\preccurlyeq y)
.

0.4.52 Example
.

Next consider
\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.68pt{\vbox to5.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84166pt\lower-2.84166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.57 -3.57 M -3.57 -3.51 L -3.57 3.51 C -3.57 3.55 -3.55 3.57 -3.51 3.57 L 3.51 3.57 C 3.55 3.57 3.57 3.55 3.57 3.51 L 3.57 -3.51 C 3.57 -3.55 3.55 -3.57 3.51 -3.57 L -3.51 -3.57 C -3.55 -3.57 -3.57 -3.55 -3.57 -3.51 Z M 3.57 3.57}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

.
The second-order translation is

\forall P\forall x(\mathsf{isfil}(P)\wedge\forall y(xRy\to Py)\to Px).

Then
\sigma(P)=\lambda u.\exists y(xRy\wedge y\preccurlyeq u)
.
Instantiating this makes the antecedent of the outer implication
vacuously true, so that we get the local correspondent

\exists y(xRy\wedge y\preccurlyeq x).

Validity of
\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to5.68pt{\vbox to5.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.84166pt\lower-2.84166pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\kern 0.86108ptp\trianglelefteqslant p
on a frame then corresponds to

\forall x\exists y(xRy\wedge y\preccurlyeq x)
.

(12)

to our logic. This is Sahlqvist, so we can use the algorithm
from Theorem
0.4.6
to the first-order frame condition
ensuring its validity.
The second-order translation is

\forall P\big(\mathsf{isfil}(P)\wedge\mathsf{isfil}(Q)\wedge\exists y(xRy\wedge(\exists z\exists z^{\prime}(\mathsf{abovemeet}(y;z,z^{\prime})\wedge Pz\wedge Qz^{\prime})))\to\st_{x}(\hskip 0.86108pt\text{\hbox to6.61pt{\vbox to6.61pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.3028pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}

Processing the formula, we obtain the following second-order translation:

\forall P\forall Q\forall x\forall y\forall z\forall z^{\prime}(\operatorname{ISFIL}\wedge xRy\wedge\mathsf{abovemeet}(y;,z,z^{\prime})\wedge Pz\wedge Qz^{\prime}\to\st_{x}(\chi))

(13)

This gives

\sigma(P)=\lambda u.z\preccurlyeq u
,
\sigma(Q)=\lambda u.z^{\prime}\preccurlyeq u
.
The standard translation of the antecedent is

\st_{x}(\chi)=\exists v\exists v^{\prime}(\mathsf{abovemeet}(x;v,v^{\prime})\wedge\exists w(vRw\wedge Pw)\wedge\exists w^{\prime}(v^{\prime}Rw^{\prime}\wedge Qw^{\prime})).

Substitution
P
and
Q
and omitting trivial terms
we obtain the following local correspondent:

\forall y\forall z\forall z^{\prime}\big((xRy\wedge(z\curlywedge z^{\prime}\preccurlyeq y))\to\exists v\exists v^{\prime}((v\curlywedge v^{\prime}\preccurlyeq x)\wedge\exists w(vRw\wedge z\preccurlyeq w)\wedge\exists w^{\prime}(v^{\prime}Rw^{\prime}\wedge z^{\prime}\preccurlyeq w^{\prime}))\big).

In a picture:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w\curlywedge w^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z\curlywedge z^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}

A modal L-frame satisfies normality of
if this
holds for all states
x
.
It is a relational analogue of the second condition
from Definition
0.2.4
that ensures preservation of joins.

0.5
Conclusion

We have given a new duality for bounded (not necessarily distributive) lattices
which resembles Stone-type dualities.
It builds on a known duality for the category of bounded
meet-semilattices given by Hofmann, Mislove and Stralka
[
32
]
.
The relation between our duality and the duality by Hofmann, Mislove and
Stralka is similar to the relation between Esakia duality and Priestley duality.
It can also be seen as a Stone-type analogue of
Jipsen and Moshier’s spectral duality for lattices
[
40
]
.

We also extended the duality to one for a modal extension
of weak positive logic with
and
.
While these are interpreted using a relation in the way as in normal
modal logic over a classical base, the non-standard interpretation of joins
causes
to no longer be join-preserving.
This interesting phenomenon has also been observed in the context of modal
intuitionistic logic
[
38
]
.

As the dualities presented in this
paper resemble known dualities used in modal logic,
they allow us to use similar tools and techniques.
To showcase this, we proved
\Pi_{1}
-persistence and Sahlqvist correspondence results
along the lines of
[
6
]
.

There are many intriguing avenues for further research, some of which we
list below.

Finite model property.

While it is easy to derive the finite model property for weak positive
logic, the same result for the modal extension
presented in this paper appears to be non-trivial.

Relation to ortho(modular) lattices.

Ortholattices and orthomodular lattices provide other interesting classes of
lattices with operators. However, in
ortholattices the orthocomplement is turning joins into meets. Duality for these structures has been discussed by Goldblatt
[
23
,
24
]
and
Bimbo
[
5
]
.
In
[
14
, Chapter 6]
the duality for lattices is extended
to account for a modal operators that turn joins into meets.
Recently modal ortholattices have been studied in
[
33
]
.
We leave it an an interesting open problem to see whether the preservation and correspondence results
of this paper can be extended to this setting.
It is also open whether these technique could be extended to orthomodular lattices
[
36
]
.
This is especially interesting as orthomodalur lattices provide
algebraic structures of quantum logic
[
13
]
, so
these methods could be relevant in the study of quantum logic.

Different modalities.

Yet another question is what other modal extensions of weak positive
logic we can define. In particular, it would be
interesting to define a form of neighbourhood semantics based on the
L-frames used in this paper and investigate the behaviour of the
resulting modalities.
